The enriched q q -monomial basis of the quasisymmetric functions
Darij Grinberg and Ekaterina A. Vassilieva

Abstract
Abstract. We construct a new family ( η α ( q ) ) α ∈ Comp \left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}} of quasisymmetric functions for each element q q of the base ring. We call them the “enriched q q -monomial quasisymmetric functions”. When r := q + 1 r:=q+1 is invertible, this family is a basis of QSym \operatorname*{QSym} . It generalizes Hoffman’s “essential quasi-symmetric functions” (obtained for q = 0 q=0 ) and Hsiao’s “monomial peak functions” (obtained for q = 1 q=1 ), but also includes the monomial quasisymmetric functions as a limiting case. We describe these functions η α ( q ) \eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)} by several formulas, and compute their products, coproducts and antipodes. The product expansion is given by an exotic variant of the shuffle product which we call the “stufufuffle product” due to its ability to pick several consecutive entries from each composition. This “stufufuffle product” has previously appeared in recent work by Bouillot, Novelli and Thibon, generalizing the “block shuffle product” from the theory of multizeta values. Keywords: quasisymmetric functions, peak algebra, shuffles, combinatorial Hopf algebras, noncommutative symmetric functions. Mathematics Subject Classification 2020: 05E05, 05A30, 11M32.



1
Introduction

Among the combinatorial Hopf algebras that consist of power series in
commuting indeterminates, one of the largest and most all-embracing is that of
the
quasisymmetric functions
, called
\operatorname*{QSym}
.
Originally introduced by Gessel in 1984
[
Gessel84
]
, it has since found
applications (e.g.) to enumerative combinatorics (
[
Sagan20
, Chapter 8]
,

[
Stanle24
, §7.19–7.23]
,
[
GesZhu18
]
), multizeta values (e.g.,

[
Hoffma15
]
), algebraic geometry (
[
Oesing18
]
) and the representation
theory of
0
-Hecke algebras (
[
Meliot17
, §6.2]
).

It was observed by Ehrenborg (
[
Ehrenb96
, Lemma 4.2]
; see

[
Biller10
, §3.3]
for a survey) that quasisymmetric
functions can also be used to encode the “flag
f
-vector” of a finite graded poset – i.e., essentially, the
number of chains over a given sequence of ranks, for each possible sequence of
ranks. Soon after, work of Bergeron, Mykytiuk, Sottile and van Willigenburg
(
[
BMSW00
, Example 5.3]
, but see
[
Biller10
, §3.4]
for an
explicit statement) showed that if the graded poset is Eulerian
(a property shared by face
posets of polytopes and simplicial spheres), then the resulting quasisymmetric
function is not arbitrary but rather belongs to a certain subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
called
Stembridge’s Hopf algebra
or the

peak algebra
or the
odd subalgebra
\Pi_{-}

of

\operatorname*{QSym}
. It was initially defined by Stembridge
[
Stembr97
, §3]
in order to find a fundamental expansion of the Schur
P
- and

Q
-functions, and has since been studied by others for related and unrelated
reasons (
[
AgBeSo14
, §6, particularly Proposition 6.5]
,
[
BMSW99
]
,

[
BMSW00
, §5]
,
[
Hsiao07
]
etc.); among other properties, it is a
Hopf subalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

Almost all bases of
\operatorname*{QSym}
constructed so far are indexed by

compositions
(i.e., tuples of positive integers), and their structure
constants are often governed by versions of shuffle products and
deconcatenation coproducts. The peak algebra is smaller, and its bases are
often indexed by
odd compositions
, i.e., compositions whose entries
are all odd. One of its simplest bases is defined as follows (for the sake of
simplicity, we use
\mathbb{Q}
as a base ring here): If
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
if
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)
is a
composition of
n
(that is, a tuple of positive integers with
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{\ell}=n
), then we define the formal power
series

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n};\\
g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in E\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}2^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}

(1)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Q}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right],

where
E\left(\alpha\right)
denotes the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}\setminus\left\{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{i}\ \mid\ 0<i<\ell\right\}
. This
\eta_{\alpha}
belongs to the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\operatorname*{QSym}
of quasisymmetric functions over
\mathbb{Q}
. If we let
\alpha
range over all
odd compositions
(i.e.,
compositions
\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)

whose entries
\alpha_{i}
are all odd), then the
\eta_{\alpha}
form a basis
of the peak algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
.

In this form, the power series
\eta_{\alpha}
have been introduced by Hsiao
(
[
Hsiao07
, Proposition 2.1]
, although his
\eta_{\alpha}
differ from
ours by a sign), who called them the
monomial peak functions
. Hsiao
computed their products, coproducts (in the sense of Hopf algebra) and
antipodes, and obtained some structural results for the peak algebra.

In this paper, we generalize the
\eta_{\alpha}
by replacing the power of
2

in (
1
) by a power of an arbitrary element
r
of the base
ring. We furthermore study the resulting quasisymmetric functions for all
compositions
\alpha
(not only for the odd ones). Thus we obtain a new family

\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
of quasisymmetric functions for each element
q
of the
base ring. When
r:=q+1
is invertible, this family is a basis of

\operatorname*{QSym}
. It generalizes Hoffman’s “essential
quasi-symmetric functions” (obtained for
q=0
) and Hsiao’s
monomial peak functions (obtained for
q=1
), but also includes the monomial
quasisymmetric functions as a limiting case.

We call our functions
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
the

enriched
q
-monomial quasisymmetric functions
. We describe
them by several formulas, and compute their products, coproducts and antipodes
(generalizing Hsiao’s results). The product expansion is the most interesting
one, as it is given by an exotic variant of the shuffle product which we call
the “stufufuffle product” due to its
ability to pick several consecutive entries from each composition. This
“stufufuffle product” has previously
appeared in recent work by Bouillot, Novelli and Thibon
[
BoNoTh22
, (1)]
,
where it was proposed as a generalization of the “block
shuffle product” from the theory of multizeta values
(
[
HirSat22
]
). While the authors of
[
BoNoTh22
]
have already found a
basis of
\operatorname*{QSym}
that multiplies according to this product,
ours is simpler and more natural. The coproduct and antipode formulas for

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
are fairly simple (the coproduct is given
by deconcatenation, whereas the antipode involves the parameter
q
being
replaced by its reciprocal
1/q
and the composition
\alpha
being reversed).
We also express the functions
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in terms of
the monomial and fundamental bases of
\operatorname*{QSym}
and vice versa.
Finally, we discuss how Hopf subalgebras of
\operatorname*{QSym}
can be
constructed by picking a subset of the set of all compositions. (This
generalizes the peak subalgebra.)

This paper is the first of (at least) two. The next shall extend the theory of
extended
P
-partitions to incorporate the parameter
q
, which will shed a
new light onto the enriched
q
-monomial quasisymmetric functions

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
while also leading to a new basis of

\operatorname*{QSym}
.

Several results in this paper have appeared (mostly without proof) in the
extended abstracts
[
GriVas21
]
and
[
GriVas22
]
.

1.1
Structure of the paper

This paper is organized as follows:

We begin by recalling the definition of quasisymmetric functions (and some
concomitant notions) in Section
2
.

Then, in Section
3
, we define the quasisymmetric functions

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
and prove their simplest properties
(conversion formulas to the
M
- and
L
-bases, formulas for antipode and
coproduct). In particular, we show that the family of these functions

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
(where
\alpha
ranges over all
compositions) forms a basis of
\operatorname*{QSym}
if and only if
r:=q+1

is invertible in the base ring.

Consequently, in Section
4
, we introduce and study the basis of

\operatorname*{NSym}
dual to this basis of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

In Section
5
, we use this to express the product
\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}
in three
equivalent ways.

Finally, we discuss some applications in Section
6
, and establish
one last formula for
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in Section

7
.

2
Quasisymmetric functions

2.1
Formal power series and quasisymmetry

We will use some of the standard notations from
[
GriRei20
, Chapter 5]
. Namely:

•

We let
\mathbb{N}=\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
.

•

We fix a commutative ring
\mathbf{k}
.

•

We consider the ring
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
of formal power series in countably many commuting
variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
. A
monomial
shall mean a formal
expression of the form
x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots
, where
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
is a sequence of nonnegative integers
such that only finitely many
\alpha_{i}
are positive. Formal power series
are formal infinite
\mathbf{k}
-linear combinations of such monomials.

•

Each monomial
x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots
has degree
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\cdots
.

•

A formal power series
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
is said to be
of bounded degree
if
there exists some
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that each monomial in
f
has degree

\leq d
(that is, each monomial of degree
>d
has coefficient
0
in
f
).

For example, the formal power series
\left(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+\cdots\right)^{3}
is of bounded degree, but the formal power series
\dfrac{1}{1-x_{1}}=1+x_{1}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}^{3}+\cdots
is not.

We shall now introduce the notion of
pack-equivalent monomials
. Let
us first illustrate it by an example:

Example 2.1
.

Question:
What do the monomials
x_{1}^{4}x_{3}^{7}x_{4}x_{9}^{2}

and
x_{3}^{4}x_{4}^{7}x_{10}x_{16}^{2}
and
x_{5}^{4}x_{6}^{7}x_{8}x_{9}^{2}
have in common (but not in common with
x_{1}^{7}x_{3}^{4}x_{4}x_{9}^{2}
) ?

Answer:
They have the same sequence of nonzero exponents (when the
variables are ordered in increasing order – i.e., if
i<j
, then
x_{i}
goes
before
x_{j}
). Or, to put it differently, they all have the form
x_{a}^{4}x_{b}^{7}x_{c}x_{d}^{2}
for some
a<b<c<d
. We shall call such pairs of
monomials
pack-equivalent
.

Let us define this concept more rigorously:

Definition 2.2
.

Two monomials
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
are said to be

pack-equivalent
if they can be written in the forms

\mathfrak{m}=x_{i_{1}}^{a_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{a_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{a_{\ell}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathfrak{n}=x_{j_{1}}^{a_{1}}x_{j_{2}}^{a_{2}}\cdots x_{j_{\ell}}^{a_{\ell}}

for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, some positive integers
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}
and two strictly increasing
\ell
-tuples
\left(i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\right)
and
\left(j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{\ell}\right)
of positive integers.

For example, the monomials
x_{1}^{4}x_{3}^{7}x_{4}x_{9}^{2}
and
x_{3}^{4}x_{4}^{7}x_{10}x_{16}^{2}
are pack-equivalent, since they can be written
as
x_{1}^{4}x_{3}^{7}x_{4}x_{9}^{2}=x_{i_{1}}^{a_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{a_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{a_{\ell}}
and
x_{3}^{4}x_{4}^{7}x_{10}x_{16}^{2}=x_{j_{1}}^{a_{1}}x_{j_{2}}^{a_{2}}\cdots x_{j_{\ell}}^{a_{\ell}}
for
\ell=4
and

\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)=\left(4,7,1,2\right)
and

\left(i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\right)=\left(1,3,4,9\right)
and

\left(j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{\ell}\right)=\left(3,4,10,16\right)
.

We are now ready to define the quasisymmetric functions:

Definition 2.3
.

(a)

A formal power series
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
is said to be

quasisymmetric
if it has the property that any two pack-equivalent
monomials have the same coefficient in
f
(that is: if
\mathfrak{m}
and

\mathfrak{n}
are two pack-equivalent monomials, then the coefficient of

\mathfrak{m}
in
f
equals the coefficient of
\mathfrak{n}
in
f
).

(b)

A
quasisymmetric function
means a formal power
series
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
that is quasisymmetric and of bounded degree.

Quasisymmetric functions have been introduced by Gessel in
[
Gessel84
]

(for
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}
at least, but the general case is not much
different). Introductions to their theory can be found in
[
GriRei20
, Chapters
5–6]
,
[
Stanle24
, §7.19]
,
[
Sagan20
, Chapter 8]
,

[
Malven93
, §4]
and various other texts.

It is known (see
[
Malven93
, Corollaire 4.7]
or

[
GriRei20
, Proposition 5.1.3]
) that the set of all
quasisymmetric functions is a
\mathbf{k}
-subalgebra of
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
. It is denoted by

\operatorname*{QSym}
and called the
ring of quasisymmetric functions
.
It has several bases (as a
\mathbf{k}
-module), most of which are indexed by compositions.

2.2
Compositions

A
composition
means a finite list
\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
of positive integers. The set of all compositions
is denoted by
\operatorname*{Comp}
. The
empty composition

\varnothing
is the composition
\left({}\right)
, which is a
0
-tuple.

The
length
\ell\left(\alpha\right)
of a composition

\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
is defined
to be the number
k
.

If
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
is a
composition, then the nonnegative integer
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}
is called the
size
of
\alpha
and is denoted by

\left|\alpha\right|
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a

composition of
n
to be a composition that has size
n
. We let

\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
be the set of all compositions of
n
(for
given
n\in\mathbb{N}
). For example,
\left(1,5,2,1\right)
is a
composition with size
9
(since
\left|\left(1,5,2,1\right)\right|=1+5+2+1=9
), so that
\left(1,5,2,1\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{9}
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
\left[n\right]
denote the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. This set is empty whenever
n\leq 0
, and otherwise
has size
n
.

It is well-known that any positive integer
n
has exactly
2^{n-1}

compositions. This has a standard bijective proof (“stars and
bars”) which is worth recalling, as the bijection itself will
be used a lot:

Definition 2.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)
be the powerset of
\left[n-1\right]
(that is, the
set of all subsets of
\left[n-1\right]
).

(a)

We define a map
D:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)
by

\displaystyle D\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{i}\ \mid\ i\in\left[k-1\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha_{1},\ \ \alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\ \ \alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3},\ \ \ldots,\ \ \alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k-1}\right\}.

(b)

We define a map
\operatorname*{comp}:\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\rightarrow\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
as
follows: For any
I\in\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)
, we
set

\operatorname*{comp}\left(I\right)=\left(i_{1}-i_{0},i_{2}-i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}-i_{m-1}\right),

where
i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}
are the elements of the set
I\cup\left\{0,n\right\}
listed in increasing order (so that
i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}
,
therefore
i_{0}=0
and
i_{m}=n
and
\left\{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{m-1}\right\}=I
).

The maps
D
and
\operatorname*{comp}
are mutually inverse bijections. (See

[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, Proposition 10.17]
for a detailed proof of this.)

For example, for
n=8
, we have
D\left(2,1,3,2\right)=\left\{2,\ 2+1,\ 2+1+3\right\}=\left\{2,3,6\right\}
and
\operatorname*{comp}\left\{2,3,6\right\}=\left(2-0,\ 3-2,\ 6-3,\ 8-6\right)=\left(2,1,3,2\right)
. Note that the meaning of
\operatorname*{comp}\left(I\right)
for a given set
I
depends on
n
, and thus the notation is
ambiguous unless
n
is specified. In contrast, the notation
D\left(\alpha\right)
is unambiguous, since
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
uniquely determines
n
to be
\left|\alpha\right|
.

The notation
D
in Definition
2.4
presumably originates in
the word “
d
escent”, but the
connection between
D
and actual descents is indirect and rather misleading.
We prefer to call
D
the “partial sum map” (as
D\left(\alpha\right)
consists of the partial sums of the
composition
\alpha
) and its inverse
\operatorname*{comp}
the
“interstitial map” (as

\operatorname*{comp}\left(I\right)
consists of the lengths of the
intervals into which the elements of
I
split the interval
\left[n\right]
). Note that Stanley, in
[
Stanle24
, §7.19]
, writes
S_{\alpha}
for

D\left(\alpha\right)
and writes
\operatorname*{co}\left(I\right)

for
\operatorname*{comp}\left(I\right)
.

Note that every composition
\alpha
of size
\left|\alpha\right|>0

satisfies
\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|=\ell\left(\alpha\right)-1
, so that
\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|+1=\ell\left(\alpha\right)
. But this fails if
\alpha
is the empty
composition
\varnothing=\left({}\right)
(since
D\left({}\right)=\varnothing
and
\ell\left({}\right)=0
).

2.3
The monomial and fundamental bases of
\operatorname*{QSym}

We will only need two bases of
\operatorname*{QSym}
: the monomial basis and
the fundamental basis.

If
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)
is a
composition, then we define the
monomial quasisymmetric function

M_{\alpha}\in\operatorname*{QSym}
by

M_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}}x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{m}\text{ is a monomial}\\
\text{pack-equivalent}\\
\text{to }x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{m}.

(2)

For example,

M_{\left(2,1\right)}=\sum\limits_{i<j}x_{i}^{2}x_{j}=x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+x_{1}^{2}x_{3}+x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+x_{1}^{2}x_{4}+x_{2}^{2}x_{4}+x_{3}^{2}x_{4}+\cdots.

The family
\left(M_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a
basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}
, and is known as the

monomial basis
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

For any composition
\alpha
, we define the
fundamental quasisymmetric
function
L_{\alpha}\in\operatorname*{QSym}
by

L_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}M_{\beta},

(3)

where
n=\left|\alpha\right|
(so that
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
). It is not hard to rewrite this
as
1
1

1

See
[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, Corollary 10.18]
for a proof
that the right hand side of (
4
) equals the right hand side
of (
3
).

L_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{n};\\
i_{j}<i_{j+1}\text{ whenever }j\in D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}

(4)

(again with
n=\left|\alpha\right|
). This quasisymmetric function

L_{\alpha}
was originally called
F_{\alpha}
in Gessel’s paper

[
Gessel84
]
(and in some later work such as
[
Malven93
]
), but the
notation
L_{\alpha}
has since spread more widely.

The family
\left(L_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a
basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}
, and is known as the

fundamental basis
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

3
The enriched
q
-monomial functions

3.1
Definition and restatements

Convention 3.1
.

From now on, we fix an element
q
of the base ring

\mathbf{k}
. We set

r:=q+1.

We shall now introduce a new family of quasisymmetric functions depending on

q
:

Definition 3.1
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any composition
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we define a quasisymmetric function

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\in\operatorname*{QSym}
by

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}.

(5)

We shall refer to
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
as the
enriched

q
-monomial function
corresponding to
\alpha
.

Example 3.2
.

(a)

Setting
n=5
and
\alpha=\left(1,3,1\right)
in this
definition, we obtain

\displaystyle\eta_{\left(1,3,1\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{5};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(1,3,1\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{5};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\left\{1,4\right\}\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }D\left(1,3,1\right)=\left\{1,4\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\ell\left(5\right)}M_{\left(5\right)}+r^{\ell\left(1,4\right)}M_{\left(1,4\right)}+r^{\ell\left(4,1\right)}M_{\left(4,1\right)}+r^{\ell\left(1,3,1\right)}M_{\left(1,3,1\right)}

(since the compositions
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{5}
satisfying

D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\left\{1,4\right\}
are
\left(5\right)
,
\left(1,4\right)
,
\left(4,1\right)
and
\left(1,3,1\right)
). This simplifies to

\eta_{\left(1,3,1\right)}^{\left(q\right)}=rM_{\left(5\right)}+r^{2}M_{\left(1,4\right)}+r^{2}M_{\left(4,1\right)}+r^{3}M_{\left(1,3,1\right)}.

(b)

For any positive integer
n
, we have

\eta_{\left(n\right)}^{\left(q\right)}=rM_{\left(n\right)},

because the only composition
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}

satisfying
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(n\right)
is the
composition
\left(n\right)
itself (since
D\left(n\right)
is the
empty set
\varnothing
) and has length
\ell\left(n\right)=1
. Likewise,
the empty composition
\varnothing=\left({}\right)
satisfies

\eta_{\varnothing}^{\left(q\right)}=M_{\varnothing}=1.

The quasisymmetric function
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
generalizes
several known power series. For
q=0
, the series
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(0\right)}
is the “essential quasi-symmetric function”
E_{I}
(for
I=D\left(\alpha\right)
) defined in
[
Hoffma15
, (8)]
. When
\alpha
is an odd
composition (i.e., all entries of
\alpha
are odd) and
q=1
, the series

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(1\right)}
is
precisely the
\eta_{\alpha}
defined in
[
AgBeSo14
, (6.1)]
, and differs
only in sign from the
\eta_{\alpha}
given in
[
Hsiao07
, (2.1)]
(because
of
[
Hsiao07
, Proposition 2.1]
). (This is the reason for the notation

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
.) Finally, in an appropriate sense, we
can view
M_{\alpha}
as the “
q\rightarrow\infty

limit” of
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
; to be
precise, this is saying that when
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
is
considered as a polynomial in
q
(over
\operatorname*{QSym}
), its leading
term is
q^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}M_{\alpha}
(which is obvious from
(
5
) and
r=q+1
).

The following two propositions are essentially restatements of
(
5
):

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Then,

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n};\\
g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}},

(6)

where the sum is over all weakly increasing
n
-tuples
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
of positive integers that satisfy
\left(g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right)
.

Proof 3.4
.

If
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
is a
weakly increasing
n
-tuple of positive integers, then we let

\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}
denote the set of all
i\in\left[n-1\right]
satisfying
g_{i}<g_{i+1}
(or, equivalently,
g_{i}\neq g_{i+1}
). Thus,

\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}
is the set of all positions at which the
entries of
\mathbf{g}
get larger (or, equivalently, change values). For
instance,
\operatorname*{Asc}\left(3,3,4,6,8,8,8\right)=\left\{2,3,4\right\}
.

Let
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Write
\beta
as

\beta=\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\right)
; thus,

\ell\left(\beta\right)=\ell
and
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{\ell}=n
and

M_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}}x_{i_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\beta_{\ell}}

(7)

(by the definition of
M_{\beta}
). Moreover, the definition of
D\left(\beta\right)
yields that

\displaystyle D\left(\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\beta_{1},\ \ \beta_{1}+\beta_{2},\ \ \beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3},\ \ \ldots,\ \ \beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{\ell-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\beta_{1}<\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}<\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}<\cdots<\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{\ell-1}\right\};

thus, the elements of
D\left(\beta\right)
subdivide the interval

\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
into
\ell
subintervals of sizes
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}
(from left to right).

A weakly increasing
n
-tuple
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfies
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
if and only if its entries are constant on each of these

\ell
subintervals (i.e., we must have
g_{i}=g_{j}
whenever
i
and
j

belong to the same subinterval) but strictly increase as we pass from one
subinterval to the next (since
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}
is the set of
all positions at which the entries of
\mathbf{g}
change values). Hence, a
weakly increasing
n
-tuple
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfies
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
if and only if it begins with
\beta_{1}
copies of a positive
integer
i_{1}
, then continues with
\beta_{2}
copies of a larger positive
integer
i_{2}
, then continues further with
\beta_{3}
copies of a
yet-larger positive integer
i_{3}
, and so on. Consequently, for any weakly
increasing
n
-tuple
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
that satisfies
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
, we can rewrite the monomial
x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
as
x_{i_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\beta_{\ell}}
using these positive integers
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
.

Conversely, any monomial of the form
x_{i_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\beta_{\ell}}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
can be rewritten in the form
x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
for a unique weakly increasing
n
-tuple
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
that satisfies

\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
(indeed, it has degree

\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{\ell}=n
, and so can be written in the form

x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
for a unique weakly increasing
n
-tuple

\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
; but then,
the property
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
follows
from the arguments in the previous paragraph).

Combining the results of the previous two paragraphs, we conclude that the
monomials
x_{i_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\beta_{\ell}}
that appear on the right hand side of
(
7
) are precisely the monomials
x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
for all weakly increasing
n
-tuples

\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfying

\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
. Therefore, we can
rewrite (
7
) as

M_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}.

(8)

Let us furthermore observe the following: If a weakly increasing
n
-tuple

\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfies

\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)
, then

\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|=\ell

(since we have proved above that the monomial
x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
can be written as
x_{i_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\beta_{\ell}}
for some integers
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
,
and thus contains exactly
\ell
distinct indeterminates, but this is saying
precisely that the set
\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}
has
exactly
\ell
distinct elements) and thus

\ell\left(\beta\right)=\ell=\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|.

(9)

Now, multiplying the equality (
8
) by

r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}
, we find

\displaystyle r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.eta.through-x.len}))}\end{subarray}}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}.

(10)

Forget that we fixed
\beta
. We thus have proved
(
10
) for each
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Now, (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.prop.eta.through-x.qlM=})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left[n-1\right];\\
I\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}=I\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }I\text{ for }D\left(\beta\right)\text{ in
the first sum,}\\
\text{since the map }D:\operatorname*{Comp}{}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right);\\
g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\left|\left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}\right|}x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}

(since the condition “
\operatorname*{Asc}\mathbf{g}\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)
” imposed on a weakly increasing

n
-tuple
\mathbf{g}=\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
is
equivalent to the condition “
g_{i}=g_{i+1}
for each

i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)
”). This proves Proposition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}
. Then,

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}}r^{\left|\left\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right\}\right|}x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}},

(11)

where the sum is over all weakly increasing
\ell
-tuples
\left(i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\right)
of positive integers.

Proof 3.6
.

Let
n=\left|\alpha\right|
, so that
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Clearly, it suffices to show that the right hand sides of
(
6
) and (
11
) are identical.

We claim that the monomials
x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}
for all

n
-tuples
2
2

2

We are no longer saying “weakly increasing”, since this
is automatically implied by the notation
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
.

\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfying
\left(g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right)
are precisely the
monomials of the form
x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}
for all
\ell
-tuples
\left(i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\right)
.

Indeed, let us set

s_{k}:=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
each }k\in\left[\ell\right].

Thus,
D\left(\alpha\right)=\left\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{\ell-1}\right\}
and
s_{\ell}=\left|\alpha\right|=n
. Hence, an

n
-tuple
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
satisfies

\left(g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right)

if and only if it satisfies

\displaystyle\ g_{1}=g_{2}=\cdots=g_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ g_{s_{1}+1}=g_{s_{1}+2}=\cdots=g_{s_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ g_{s_{2}+1}=g_{s_{2}+2}=\cdots=g_{s_{3}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \cdots

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ g_{s_{\ell-1}+1}=g_{s_{\ell-1}+2}=\cdots=g_{s_{\ell}}

(that is, if its entries can grow only at the positions
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{\ell-1}
). Therefore, such an
n
-tuple is uniquely determined by
its “essential” entries
g_{s_{1}},g_{s_{2}},\ldots,g_{s_{\ell}}
. More precisely, there is a bijection from the
set

\left\{n\text{-tuples }\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)\text{ satisfying }\left(g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right\}

to the set

\left\{\ell\text{-tuples }\left(i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\right)\right\},

which sends each
n
-tuple
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
from the former set to its “subword”
\left(g_{s_{1}}\leq g_{s_{2}}\leq\cdots\leq g_{s_{\ell}}\right)

3
3

3

This bijection is precisely the
bijection
\Phi
from
[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, proof of Proposition
10.69]
. Indeed, our set

\left\{n\text{-tuples }\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)\text{ satisfying }\left(g_{i}=g_{i+1}\text{ for each }i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right\}

is the set
\mathcal{I}
from
[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, proof of Proposition
10.69]
(since the condition “
g_{i}=g_{i+1}
for
each
i\in\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)
” can be rewritten as “
\left\{j\in\left[n-1\right]\ \mid\ g_{j}<g_{j+1}\right\}\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)
”), whereas our set

\left\{\ell\text{-tuples }\left(i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\right)\right\}

is the set
\mathcal{J}
from
[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, proof of Proposition
10.69]
.
. This bijection has the property that

x_{g_{1}}x_{g_{2}}\cdots x_{g_{n}}=x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left\{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\right\}=\left\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell}\right\}

whenever it sends an
n
-tuple
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
to the
\ell
-tuple
\left(i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\right)=\left(g_{s_{1}}\leq g_{s_{2}}\leq\cdots\leq g_{s_{\ell}}\right)
(because the
n
-tuple
\left(g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}\right)
consists of
\alpha_{1}
copies of
i_{1}
, followed by

\alpha_{2}
copies of
i_{2}
, followed by
\alpha_{3}
copies of
i_{3}
,
and so on). This shows that the right hand sides of the equalities
(
6
) and (
11
) are
identical. Hence, (
11
) follows from
(
6
). This proves Proposition

3.5
.

3.2
The
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
as a basis

The equality (
5
) writes each enriched
q
-monomial
function
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
as a
\mathbf{k}
-linear
combination of
M_{\beta}
’s. Conversely, we can expand each monomial
quasisymmetric function
M_{\beta}
as a
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
’s, at least after multiplying it by

r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}
:

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
be a composition. Then,

r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

For the proof of this proposition (and some later ones as well), we will need
the
Iverson bracket notation
:

Convention 3.2
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a logical statement, then
\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
shall denote the truth value of
\mathcal{A}
(that is,
the number
1
if
\mathcal{A}
is true, and the number
0
if
\mathcal{A}

is false).

For example,
\left[2+2=4\right]=1
and
\left[2+2=5\right]=0
.

The following lemma is a classical elementary property of finite sets, but we
recall its proof for the sake of completeness:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
S
and
T
be two finite sets. Then,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|S\right|-\left|I\right|}=\left[S=T\right].

Proof 3.9

(Proof.)
.

If
T\not\subseteq S
, then the left hand side is an empty sum and
thus equals
0
, as does the right hand side. Hence, for the rest of this
proof, we WLOG assume that
T\subseteq S
. Hence,
S\setminus T=\varnothing

holds if and only if
S=T
. Therefore,
\left[S\setminus T=\varnothing\right]=\left[S=T\right]
.

Each subset
I
of
S
can be uniquely written as
S\setminus J
for a unique
subset
J
of
S
(namely, this
J
is the complement of
I
in
S
). Thus, we
can reindex the sum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|S\right|-\left|I\right|}
by
substituting
S\setminus J
for
I
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|S\right|-\left|I\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq S\setminus J\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|S\right|-\left|S\setminus J\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|J\right|}\\
\text{(since }\left|S\right|-\left|S\setminus J\right|=\left|J\right|\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq S\setminus J\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|J\right|}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq S;\\
J\subseteq S\setminus T\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|J\right|}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the condition \textquotedblleft}T\subseteq S\setminus J\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{is equivalent to \textquotedblleft}J\subseteq S\setminus T\text{\textquotedblright\ when}\\
\text{both }J\text{ and }T\text{ are subsets of }S\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq S\setminus T}\left(-1\right)^{\left|J\right|}

(15)

(since each subset
J\subseteq S\setminus T
satisfies
J\subseteq S
).

However, a known fact about finite sets (see, e.g.,
[
Grinbe20
, Proposition
2.9.10]
) says that
\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}=\left[S=\varnothing\right]
. Applying this
fact to
S\setminus T
instead of
S
, we obtain
\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S\setminus T}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}=\left[S\setminus T=\varnothing\right]=\left[S=T\right]
. Thus, (
15
)
becomes

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
T\subseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|S\right|-\left|I\right|}=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq S\setminus T}\left(-1\right)^{\left|J\right|}=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S\setminus T}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}=\left[S=T\right].

This proves Lemma
3.8
.

We will also use the following property of compositions:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

(a)

We have
\ell\left(\delta\right)=\left|D\left(\delta\right)\right|+\left[n\neq 0\right]
for each
\delta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
.

(b)

We have
\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)=\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|-\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|
for any
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
and
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
.

Proof 3.11
.

(a)
Part
(a)
follows easily from the definition of
D\left(\delta\right)
. For a detailed proof, see
[
GriVas23
, Corollary 2.6]
.

(b)
Use part
(a)
. For a detailed proof, see
[
GriVas23
, Corollary
2.7]
.

Proof 3.12

(Proof of Proposition
3.7
.)
.

For each
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we have

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}

(by (
5
), with
\beta
renamed as
\gamma
). Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}.

(16)

However, for each
\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|-\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.comps.l-vs-size} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|-\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left[n-1\right];\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq I\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|-\left|I\right|}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }I\text{ for }D\left(\alpha\right)\text{
in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }D:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq D\left(\beta\right);\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|-\left|I\right|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\left[n-1\right]\text{, so
that each subset }I\\
\text{of }D\left(\beta\right)\text{ is also a subset of }\left[n-1\right]\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[D\left(\beta\right)=D\left(\gamma\right)\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.altsum.1}, applied to
}S=D\left(\beta\right)\text{ and }T=D\left(\gamma\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\beta=\gamma\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the
map }D\text{ is a bijection}\right).

Hence, (
16
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\beta\right)-\ell\left(\alpha\right)}}_{=\left[\beta=\gamma\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}r^{\ell\left(\gamma\right)}M_{\gamma}\left[\beta=\gamma\right]=r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

(since the factor
\left[\beta=\gamma\right]
in the sum ensures that the
only nonzero addend in the sum is the addend for
\gamma=\beta
). This proves
Proposition
3.7
.

Proposition
3.7
shows that the quasisymmetric
functions
r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}
for all
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}
are
\mathbf{k}
-linear combinations of the enriched

q
-monomial quasisymmetric functions
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
. If

r
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
, then it follows that the monomial
quasisymmetric functions
M_{\beta}
are such combinations as well, and thus
the family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
spans the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}

in this case. But we can actually say more:

Theorem 3.13
.

Assume that
r
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
. Then:

(a)

The family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

(b)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the
n
-th graded component

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}
of the graded
\mathbf{k}
-module

\operatorname*{QSym}
. Then, the family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
is a basis
of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}
.

Proof 3.14
.

(b)
Define a partial order
\prec
on the finite set

\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
by setting

\beta\prec\alpha\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{if and only if}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ell\left(\beta\right)<\ell\left(\alpha\right).

If two compositions
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
satisfy

D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)
and
\beta\neq\alpha
, then
D\left(\beta\right)
is a proper subset of
D\left(\alpha\right)
(since
D
is a bijection, so that
\beta\neq\alpha
entails

D\left(\beta\right)\neq D\left(\alpha\right)
), and thus we have

\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|<\left|D\left(\alpha\right)\right|
and therefore
\ell\left(\beta\right)<\ell\left(\alpha\right)
(by Lemma
3.10

(b)
) and thus

\beta\prec\alpha

(17)

(by the definition of the partial order
\prec
).

For each
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}M_{\alpha}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right);\\
\beta\neq\alpha\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=r^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}M_{\alpha}+\left(\text{a linear
combination of }M_{\beta}\text{ with }\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\text{ satisfying }\beta\prec\alpha\right)

(by (
17
)). Since
r
is invertible, this shows
that the family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
expands invertibly triangularly
in the family
\left(M_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
with respect to the partial order
\prec
(where we are using
the terminology from
[
GriRei20
, §11.1]
). Hence, a classical fact about
triangular expansions (
[
GriRei20
, Corollary 11.1.19(e)]
) shows that the
family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}
(since the family
\left(M_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
is a basis of

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}
). This proves Theorem
3.13

(b)
.

(a)
Theorem
3.13

(b)
says that the family

\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, the
family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{n}=\operatorname*{QSym}
. This
proves Theorem
3.13

(a)
.

Theorem
3.13

(a)
has a converse: If the family

\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a basis of
\operatorname*{QSym}
, then
r
is
invertible. (This is already clear from considering its unique degree-
1

entry
\eta_{\left(1\right)}^{\left(q\right)}=rM_{\left(1\right)}
.)

3.3
Relation to the fundamental basis

We can also expand the
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in the fundamental
basis and vice versa:

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Then,

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. Then,

r^{n}L_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(D\left(\gamma\right)\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

Note that Proposition
3.15
generalizes
[
Hsiao07
, Proposition
2.2]
.

Both propositions can be proved by the help of a rather simple
identity:
4
4

4

We will use Convention
3.2
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
S
and
T
be two finite sets. Then,

\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}=\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}.

Proof 3.18

(Proof of Lemma
3.17
.)
.

We are in one of the following
two cases:

Case 1:
We have
S\subseteq T
.

Case 2:
We have
S\not\subseteq T
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
S\subseteq T
. Hence,

\left[S\subseteq T\right]=1
, so that
\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=1\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=r^{\left|S\right|}
.

Also, each subset
I
of
S
satisfies
I\subseteq S\subseteq T
and therefore
satisfies
I\setminus T=\varnothing
and
I\cap T=I
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|\varnothing\right|}q^{\left|I\right|}\\
\text{(since }I\setminus T=\varnothing\text{ and }I\cap T=I\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|\varnothing\right|}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{0}=1}q^{\left|I\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}q^{\left|I\right|}.

(18)

Now, consider the sum
\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}q^{\left|I\right|}
on the
right hand side. This sum contains
\dbinom{\left|S\right|}{k}

copies of each possible power
q^{k}
(since the number of
k
-element subsets
of
S
is
\dbinom{\left|S\right|}{k}
), and thus can be rewritten
as
\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\left|S\right|}\dbinom{\left|S\right|}{k}q^{k}
. Hence, (
18
) rewrites as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\left|S\right|}\dbinom{\left|S\right|}{k}q^{k}

\displaystyle=\left(1+q\right)^{\left|S\right|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the binomial formula}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\left|S\right|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}1+q=q+1=r\right)

\displaystyle=\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=r^{\left|S\right|}\right).

Thus, Lemma
3.17
is proved in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
S\not\subseteq T
. Hence,

\left[S\subseteq T\right]=0
, so that
\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=0\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=0
.

There exists some
s\in S
such that
s\notin T
(since
S\not\subseteq T
).
Consider this
s
. Now, each subset
I
of
S
satisfies either
s\in I
or

s\notin I
(but not both). Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\in I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|\left(I\cup\left\{s\right\}\right)\setminus T\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|+1}\\
\text{(since }s\notin T\text{ and }s\notin I\text{, so}\\
\text{that
}\left|\left(I\cup\left\{s\right\}\right)\setminus T\right|=\left|I\setminus T\right|+1\text{)}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{q^{\left|\left(I\cup\left\{s\right\}\right)\cap T\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}\\
\text{(since }s\notin T\text{
and}\\
\text{thus }\left(I\cup\left\{s\right\}\right)\cap T=I\cap T\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }I\cup\left\{s\right\}\text{ for }I\text{
in the first sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\left\{I\subseteq S\ \mid\ s\notin I\right\}\rightarrow\left\{I\subseteq S\ \mid\ s\in I\right\}\\
\text{that sends each }I\text{ to }I\cup\left\{s\right\}\text{ is a
bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|+1}}_{=-\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq S;\\
s\notin I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}=0=\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}

(since
\left[S\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|S\right|}=0
).
Thus, Lemma
3.17
is proved in Case 2. The proof of
Lemma
3.17
is thus complete.

Proof 3.19

(Proof of Proposition
3.15
.)
.

We begin by observing that

\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+1=\ell\left(\beta\right)

(19)

for every
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}

5
5

5

Proof.
Let
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. From
n\neq 0
(since
n
is positive), we obtain
\left[n\neq 0\right]=1
. However, Lemma
3.10

(a)

(applied to
\delta=\beta
) yields
\ell\left(\beta\right)=\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+\underbrace{\left[n\neq 0\right]}_{=1}=\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+1
. This proves
(
19
).
.

Let
T:=D\left(\alpha\right)
. Thus,
D\left(\alpha\right)=T
, so that

\displaystyle r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap T\right|}\underbrace{L_{\gamma}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}\\
\text{(by the definition of }L_{\gamma}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap T\right|}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap T\right|}M_{\beta}.

However, every
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left[n-1\right];\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq I\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }I\text{ for }D\left(\gamma\right)\text{
in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }D:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq D\left(\beta\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\left[n-1\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left[D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|}

(by Lemma
3.17
, applied to
S=D\left(\beta\right)
). Hence, this becomes

\displaystyle r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap T\right|}}_{=\left[D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|}}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left[D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|}M_{\beta}=r\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\end{subarray}}r^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\end{subarray}}\underbrace{r^{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.eta.through-F.short.Dl}))}\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq T\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }T=D\left(\alpha\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the
definition of }\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right).

This proves Proposition
3.15
.

Proof 3.20

(Proof of Proposition
3.16
.)
.

For each subset
J
of

\left[n-1\right]
, we let
\overline{J}
denote its complement
\left[n-1\right]\setminus J
. It is easy to see that

\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|=n-\ell\left(\beta\right)

(20)

for every
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}

6
6

6

Proof.
Let
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
. From
n\neq 0
(since
n
is positive), we obtain
\left[n\neq 0\right]=1
. However, Lemma
3.10

(a)

(applied to
\delta=\beta
) yields
\ell\left(\beta\right)=\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+\underbrace{\left[n\neq 0\right]}_{=1}=\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|+1
, so that
\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|=\ell\left(\beta\right)-1
. However,
the definition of
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}
yields
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}=\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\beta\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\beta\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left|\left[n-1\right]\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=n-1\\
\text{(since }n-1\in\mathbb{N}\text{)}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\left|D\left(\beta\right)\right|}_{=\ell\left(\beta\right)-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\left[n-1\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(n-1\right)-\left(\ell\left(\beta\right)-1\right)=n-\ell\left(\beta\right).

This proves (
20
).
.

Let
T:=\overline{D\left(\gamma\right)}
. Thus,
D\left(\gamma\right)=\overline{T}
, so that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(D\left(\gamma\right)\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}\underbrace{\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}.

However, every
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq\left[n-1\right];\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq K\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus K\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup K\right)\right|}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }K\text{ for }D\left(\alpha\right)\text{
in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }D:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left[n-1\right];\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\overline{I}\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}}\\
\text{(since the subsets }I\text{ of
}\left[n-1\right]\\
\text{satisfying }D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq\overline{I}\text{ are precisely}\\
\text{the subsets of }\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus\overline{I}\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}\\
\text{(since }\overline{T}\setminus\overline{I}=I\setminus T\text{)}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup\overline{I}\right)\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}\\
\text{(since we
have}\\
\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup\overline{I}\right)=\overline{\overline{T}\cup\overline{I}}=\overline{\overline{I}\cup\overline{T}}=I\cap T\\
\text{by de Morgan's laws)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\overline{I}\text{ for }K\text{ in the sum,
since}\\
\text{the map }\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\left[n-1\right]\right)\text{ that sends each}\\
\text{subset }I\text{ to its complement }\overline{I}\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\setminus T\right|}q^{\left|I\cap T\right|}=\left[\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|}

(by Lemma
3.17
, applied to
S=\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}
). Hence, this becomes

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(D\left(\gamma\right)\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\overline{T}\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|\left[n-1\right]\setminus\left(\overline{T}\cup D\left(\alpha\right)\right)\right|}}_{=\left[\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left[\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\right]\cdot r^{\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{r^{\left|\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=r^{n-\ell\left(\beta\right)}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.eta.F-through.size-of-comp}))}\end{subarray}}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\end{subarray}}\underbrace{r^{n-\ell\left(\beta\right)}r^{\ell\left(\beta\right)}}_{=r^{n}}M_{\beta}=r^{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq T\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=r^{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq\overline{D\left(\gamma\right)}\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}T=\overline{D\left(\gamma\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{n}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\end{subarray}}M_{\beta}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=L_{\gamma}\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.Lalpha.def}), applied
to }\alpha=\gamma\text{)}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the condition \textquotedblleft}\overline{D\left(\beta\right)}\subseteq\overline{D\left(\gamma\right)}\text{\textquotedblright\ on a}\\
\text{composition }\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\text{ is
equivalent}\\
\text{to the condition \textquotedblleft}D\left(\beta\right)\supseteq D\left(\gamma\right)\text{\textquotedblright}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{n}L_{\gamma}.

This proves Proposition
3.16
.

3.4
The antipode of
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

The
antipode
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
is a certain
\mathbf{k}
-linear
map
S:\operatorname*{QSym}\rightarrow\operatorname*{QSym}
that can be
defined in terms of the Hopf algebra structure of
\operatorname*{QSym}
,
which we have not defined so far. But there are various formulas for its
values on certain quasisymmetric functions that can be used as alternative
definitions. For example, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we have

S\left(M_{\alpha}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{\ell}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n};\\
D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\alpha_{\ell},\alpha_{\ell-1},\ldots,\alpha_{1}\right)\end{subarray}}M_{\gamma}.

(21)

This formula (which appears, e.g., in
[
Malven93
, (4.26)]
7
7

7

The proof given in
[
Malven93
]
requires
\mathbf{k}
to be a

\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, but it is easy to see that the truth of (
21
)
for
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Q}
implies the truth of (
21
) for every
commutative ring
\mathbf{k}
.
and in
[
GriRei20
, Theorem 5.1.11]
8
8

8

Note that
[
GriRei20
, Theorem 5.1.11]
uses the notation

\operatorname*{rev}\alpha
for the composition
\left(\alpha_{\ell},\alpha_{\ell-1},\ldots,\alpha_{1}\right)
, and writes “
\gamma
coarsens
\operatorname*{rev}\alpha
” for what we
call “
\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
and

D\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)
”.
or in
[
Grinbe15
, detailed version, Proposition 10.70]
) can be used to define
S
(since
S
is to be
\mathbf{k}
-linear). Also, for each composition
\alpha
, we have
S\left(L_{\alpha}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{\left|\alpha\right|}L_{\omega\left(\alpha\right)}
, where
\omega\left(\alpha\right)
is a certain
composition known as the
conjugate
of
\alpha
. See

[
Malven93
, Corollaire 4.20]
or
[
GriRei20
, Theorem
5.1.11 and Proposition 5.2.15]
for details and proofs. Note that
S

is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism and an involution (that is,

S^{2}=\operatorname*{id}
). (Again, this is derived from abstract algebraic
properties of antipodes in
[
GriRei20
]
, but can also be showed more directly.)

We will prove two formulas for the antipode of
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
. Both rely on the following notation:

Definition 3.21
.

If
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\right)
is a composition, then the
reversal
of
\alpha
is defined
to be the composition
\left(\alpha_{\ell},\alpha_{\ell-1},\ldots,\alpha_{1}\right)
. It is denoted by
\operatorname*{rev}\alpha
.

We are now ready to state our first formula for the antipode of
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in the case when
q
is invertible:

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
p\in\mathbf{k}
be such that
pq=1
. Let
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
, and let
n=\left|\alpha\right|
. Then, the
antipode
S
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
satisfies

S\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)=\left(-q\right)^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\eta_{\operatorname*{rev}\alpha}^{\left(p\right)}.

Proof 3.23
.

From
pq=1
, we obtain
p=q^{-1}
. Furthermore,
\left(p+1\right)q=\underbrace{pq}_{=1}+q=1+q=q+1=r
. Solving this for
p+1
, we obtain

p+1=rq^{-1}.

(22)

We shall need a few more features of compositions. For any composition

\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
, we let
\omega\left(\gamma\right)
denote the unique composition of
n
satisfying

D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)=\left[n-1\right]\setminus D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\gamma\right).

(23)

(This
\omega\left(\gamma\right)
is indeed unique, since the map
D
is a
bijection.) Then, a classical formula (
[
Malven93
, (4.27)]
or

[
GriRei20
, (5.2.7)]
) says that each

\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
satisfies

S\left(L_{\gamma}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{n}L_{\omega\left(\gamma\right)}.

(24)

It is also easy to prove (see, e.g.,
[
GriVas23
, Proposition 4.3
(d)
]
) that

\omega\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)=\gamma\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}.

(25)

Thus, the map
\omega:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
(which sends each
\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
to
\omega\left(\gamma\right)
) is a bijection.

We WLOG assume that
n\neq 0
(since the claim of Theorem
3.22
is
easily checked by hand in the case when
n=0
).

From
n=\left|\alpha\right|
, we obtain
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
.

Now, we make the following combinatorial observation:

Observation 1:
Let
\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}
.
Then,

\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|=\ell\left(\alpha\right)-1-\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|

(26)

and

\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|=n-\ell\left(\alpha\right)-\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|.

(27)

The proof of Observation 1 is laborious but fairly straightforward, and can be
found in
[
GriVas23
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Now, Proposition
3.15
(applied to
\operatorname*{rev}\alpha
,
p
and
p+1
instead of
\alpha
,
q
and
r
) yields

\eta_{\operatorname*{rev}\alpha}^{\left(p\right)}=\left(p+1\right)\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|}p^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}.

(28)

On the other hand, Proposition
3.15
yields

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}.

Applying the map
S
to both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle S\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=S\left(r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}L_{\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}\underbrace{S\left(L_{\gamma}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{n}L_{\omega\left(\gamma\right)}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.eta.S.SLgam}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the map }S\\
\text{is }\mathbf{k}\text{-linear}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}\left(-1\right)^{n}L_{\omega\left(\gamma\right)}

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}\left(-1\right)^{n}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{n}\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}q^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}}\underbrace{L_{\omega\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=L_{\gamma}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.eta.S.oo=id}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\omega\left(\gamma\right)\text{ for
}\gamma\text{ in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\omega:\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}\text{ is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=r\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\operatorname*{Comp}\nolimits_{n}}\left(-1\right)^{n}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\setminus D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{n-\ell\left(\alpha\right)-\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\setminus D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.eta.S.O1.2}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{q^{\left|D\left(\omega\left(\gamma\right)\right)\cap D\left(\alpha\right)\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=q^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)-1-\left|D\left(\gamma\right)\cap D\left(\operatorname*{rev}\alpha\right)\right|}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.eta.S.O1.1}))}\end{subarray}}L_{\gamma}
