\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|=\alpha_{s}\text{ for all }s\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}\eta_{\delta}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\ast\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\delta,\varepsilon\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|=\alpha_{s}\text{ for all
}s\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.product.1.dq=}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \eta_{\delta}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\ast\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\delta,\varepsilon\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\delta}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\ast\left(q\right)}.

Thus, Claim 1 is proved.

Claim 2:
Let
\delta
and
\varepsilon
be two compositions. If
r

is invertible, then

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

Proof 5.10

(Proof of Claim 2.)
.

Essentially, this follows by duality (Lemma

5.4
) from Claim 1. Here are the details:

Assume that
r
is invertible. For any composition
\alpha
, we have

\Delta\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)}

(86)

(by Claim 1, with the letters
\delta
and
\varepsilon
renamed as
\lambda

and
\mu
).

Let
I
be the set
\operatorname*{Comp}\times\operatorname*{Comp}
. Then, we
can rewrite (
86
) as follows: For any composition

\alpha
, we have

\Delta\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\right)=\sum\limits_{\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\in I}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)}.

(87)

Then, Theorem
3.13

(a)
shows that the family
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}

is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\operatorname*{QSym}
. In other words,
the family
\left(\eta_{\beta}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module

\operatorname*{QSym}
. Hence, we can write the quasisymmetric function

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\in\operatorname*{QSym}
as

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\eta_{\beta}^{\left(q\right)},

(88)

where
\left(c_{\beta}\right)_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\in\mathbf{k}^{\operatorname*{Comp}}
is a family of coefficients (with

c_{\beta}=0
for all but finitely many
\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}
).
Consider this family.

For every
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\eta_{\beta}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.product.c2.pf.1})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\underbrace{\left\langle\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\eta_{\beta}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\alpha=\beta\right]\\
\text{(by
(\ref{eq.prop.etastar-dual-basis.duality}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\left[\alpha=\beta\right]=c_{\alpha}

(since all addends of the sum
\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\left[\alpha=\beta\right]
except for the
\beta=\alpha
addend are
0
)
and therefore

\displaystyle c_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\in I}}_{=\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}}\underbrace{\left\langle d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle}_{=d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\left\langle\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle}\left\langle\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.NSym-duality.2inf}, applied to }f=\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\text{ and }g=\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\\
\text{and }h=\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\text{ and }s_{\left(\lambda,\mu\right)}=d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\text{ and }t_{\left(\lambda,\mu\right)}=\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\\
\text{(since (\ref{pf.thm.product.c2.pf.DeltaI}) yields }\Delta\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\right)=\sum\limits_{\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\in I}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\otimes\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)}\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\underbrace{\left\langle\eta_{\lambda}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\lambda=\delta\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.prop.etastar-dual-basis.duality}))}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left\langle\eta_{\mu}^{\ast\left(q\right)},\ \eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\mu=\varepsilon\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.prop.etastar-dual-basis.duality}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\underbrace{\left[\lambda=\delta\right]\cdot\left[\mu=\varepsilon\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\lambda=\delta\text{ and }\mu=\varepsilon\right]\\
=\left[\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\left(\delta,\varepsilon\right)\right]\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\left[\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\left(\delta,\varepsilon\right)\right]

\displaystyle=d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)

(92)

(since all addends of the sum
\sum\limits_{\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\lambda,\mu}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\left[\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\left(\delta,\varepsilon\right)\right]
except for the
\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\left(\delta,\varepsilon\right)
addend are
0
).

Now, (
88
) becomes

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\operatorname*{Comp}}c_{\beta}\eta_{\beta}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\underbrace{c_{\alpha}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.product.c2.pf.4}))}\end{subarray}}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

This proves Claim 2.

In the rest of this proof, we will use several different base rings. Thus, we
shall use the notation
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
for what
we have previously been calling
\operatorname*{QSym}
(that is, the ring of
quasisymmetric functions over the ring
\mathbf{k}
). Clearly, any ring
homomorphism
f:\mathbf{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{l}
between two commutative rings

\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{l}
canonically induces a ring homomorphism

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}\rightarrow\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{l}}
, which we denote by
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}
. Moreover, if
\mathbf{k}
is a subring of a commutative ring

\mathbf{l}
, then
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
canonically
becomes a subring of
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{l}}
.

We note that the definition of the power series
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
does not depend on the base ring. Thus, if
f:\mathbf{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{l}
is a ring homomorphism between two commutative rings

\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{l}
, then any
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
and
any
q\in\mathbf{k}
satisfy

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(f\left(q\right)\right)}

(93)

(where the
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
on the left hand side is
defined in
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
, whereas the

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(f\left(q\right)\right)}
on the right hand side
is defined in
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{l}}
). Likewise, if

\mathbf{k}
is a subring of
\mathbf{l}
, then the
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
equals the

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
in
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{l}}
. We shall use this tacitly soon.

Claim 3:
Let
\delta
and
\varepsilon
be two compositions. If

\mathbf{k}
is the polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
, and if
q

is the indeterminate
X
in this ring, then

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

In other words, we have

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(X\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(X\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in
}\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}.

(94)

Proof 5.11

(Proof of Claim 3.)
.

Consider the field
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)
of
rational functions in
X
over
\mathbb{Q}
. Clearly,
\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
is a subring of
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)
. Thus,

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}
becomes a
subring of
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)}
.

In the ring
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)
, the polynomial
X+1
is
invertible. Thus, Claim 2 (applied to
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)
,
X
and

X+1
instead of
\mathbf{k}
,
q
and
r
) yields that

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(X\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(X\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in
}\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)}.

(95)

But
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}
is a
subring of
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)}
, and
both sides of the equality (
95
) belong to

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}
(since

d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\in\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
and
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\in\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}
for all
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
), and do not depend on the base ring
17
17

17

Indeed,
the power series
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}
defined over

\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
equals the power series
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\right)
.
. Hence, the
equality (
95
) holds in
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}
as well. In other words, we have

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(X\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(X\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in
}\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}.

In other words, (
94
) holds. This proves Claim 3.

Claim 4:
Let
\delta
and
\varepsilon
be two compositions. Then,

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

Proof 5.12

(Proof of Claim 4.)
.

Consider the polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
. By the universal property of a polynomial ring, there exists a unique

\mathbb{Z}
-algebra homomorphism
f:\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
that sends
X
to
q
. Consider this
f
. Explicitly,

f
is given by

f\left(u\left(X\right)\right)=u\left(q\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any polynomial }u\left(X\right)\in\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right].

(96)

The map
f
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra homomorphism, thus a ring homomorphism,
and therefore induces a ring homomorphism
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}:\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]}\rightarrow\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}
. Applying this ring homomorphism

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}
to both sides of
(
94
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(\eta_{\delta}^{\left(X\right)}\right)\cdot\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\right)\cdot\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in }\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{\mathbf{k}}.

Since every composition
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(f\left(X\right)\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.thm.product.feta})}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}f\left(X\right)=q\right),

we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\underbrace{\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\left(d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=f\left(d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\right)\\
\text{(since the homomorphism }\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{f}\\
\text{acts as }f\text{ on }\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\underbrace{f\left(d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.product.c4.fuX}))}\end{subarray}}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

This proves Claim 4.

Claim 5:
Let
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
,
\delta
and
\varepsilon
be three compositions. Then,

\displaystyle d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}.

(97)

Proof 5.13

(Proof of Claim 5.)
.

The condition “
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|=\alpha_{s}
for all

s
” under the summation sign in (
84
) is equivalent to the condition “
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)
” (since
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
). Thus, we can replace the former condition in
(
84
) by the latter. The result of this replacement is
precisely the equality (
97
). Hence, Claim 5 is proved.

Now, Theorem
5.1
is just a restatement of Claim 4. Indeed, let

\delta
and
\varepsilon
be two compositions. Then,

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\\
\text{(since any composition}\\
\text{has a unique length)}\end{subarray}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim 4}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}\right)\cdot\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.thm.product.1.dq=3})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\underbrace{\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\eta_{\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\\
\text{(since }\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all }s;\\
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\eta_{\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

(98)

However, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the following equality of
summation signs:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for
all }s;\\
\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all
}s;\\
\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the condition \textquotedblleft}\alpha=\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{under the second summation sign uniquely determines }\alpha\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all
}s;\\
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|>0\text{ for all }s\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the condition}\\
\text{\textquotedblleft}\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{is equivalent to \textquotedblleft}\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|>0\text{ for all }s\text{\textquotedblright}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for all
}s;\\
\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)>0\text{
for all }s\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the condition \textquotedblleft}\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|>0\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{on two compositions }\beta_{s}\text{ and }\gamma_{s}\\
\text{is equivalent to \textquotedblleft}\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)>0\text{\textquotedblright}\\
\text{(indeed, both conditions are}\\
\text{equivalent to }\left(\beta_{s},\gamma_{s}\right)\neq\left(\varnothing,\varnothing\right)\text{)}\end{array}\right).

Hence, we can rewrite (
98
) as

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for
all }s;\\
\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)>0\text{ for all }s\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\eta_{\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

Combining the two summation signs here into a single sum, we can rewrite this
as

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N};\\
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};\\
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta;\\
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;\\
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1\text{ for
all }s;\\
\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)>0\text{ for all }s\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\eta_{\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

Thus, Theorem
5.1
is proved.

5.2
The product rule in terms of stufflers

We will next rewrite Theorem
5.1
in a somewhat different
language, using certain surjective maps instead of factorizations of
compositions. First, we introduce several pieces of notation:

Definition 5.14
.

Let
i
and
j
be two integers. Then, we write
i\approx j
(and say that
i

is
nearly equal
to
j
) if and only if
\left|i-j\right|\leq 1
.

(Of course,
\approx
is not an equivalence relation.)

Definition 5.15
.

Let
\delta=\left(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m}\right)
be two compositions.

Fix two chains (i.e., totally ordered sets)
P=\left\{p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots<p_{\ell}\right\}
and
Q=\left\{q_{1}<q_{2}<\cdots<q_{m}\right\}
, and let

U=P\sqcup Q

be their disjoint union. This
U
is a poset with
\ell+m
elements

p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell},q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{m}
, whose relations are
given by
p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots<p_{\ell}
and
q_{1}<q_{2}<\cdots<q_{m}
(while
each
p_{i}
is incomparable to each
q_{j}
).

If
f:U\rightarrow X
is a map from
U
to any set
X
, and if
s\in X
is any
element, then we define the two sets

\displaystyle f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{u\in\left[\ell\right]\ \mid\ f\left(p_{u}\right)=s\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v\in\left[m\right]\ \mid\ f\left(q_{v}\right)=s\right\}.

(Essentially,
f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)
and
f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)
are the sets of the preimages of
s
in
P
and
Q
, respectively, except
that they consist of numbers instead of actual elements of
P
and
Q
.)

A
stufufuffler
for
\delta
and
\varepsilon
shall mean a surjective
and weakly order-preserving map

f:U\rightarrow\left\{1<2<\cdots<k\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
some }k\in\mathbb{N}

with the property that each
s\in\left\{1<2<\cdots<k\right\}
satisfies

\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|\approx\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|.

(99)

(“Weakly order-preserving” means that if

u
and
v
are two elements of the poset
U
satisfying
u<v
, then
f\left(u\right)\leq f\left(v\right)
.)

If
f:U\rightarrow\left\{1<2<\cdots<k\right\}
is a stufufuffler for

\delta
and
\varepsilon
, then we define three further concepts:

•

We define the
weight

\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
of

f
to be the composition
\left(\operatorname*{wt}\nolimits_{1}\left(f\right),\operatorname*{wt}\nolimits_{2}\left(f\right),\ldots,\operatorname*{wt}\nolimits_{k}\left(f\right)\right)
, where

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\nolimits_{s}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{u\in f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)}\delta_{u}+\sum\limits_{v\in f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)}\varepsilon_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\left[\ell\right];\\
f\left(p_{u}\right)=s\end{subarray}}\delta_{u}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\left[m\right];\\
f\left(q_{v}\right)=s\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{v}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }s\in\left[k\right].

(Note that (
99
) ensures that the two sums on
the right hand side here have nearly equal numbers of addends. Moreover, the
surjectivity of
f
ensures that at least one of these two sums has at least
one addend, and thus
\operatorname*{wt}\nolimits_{s}\left(f\right)
is a
positive integer; therefore,
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
is a composition.)

•

We define the
loss
of
f
to be the nonnegative integer

\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right):=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|,\ \ \left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|\right\}-k.

(This really is a nonnegative integer, since the surjectivity of
f
yields
that
\max\left\{\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|,\ \ \left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|\right\}\geq 1
for
each
s\in\left[k\right]
, and thus
\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\underbrace{\max\left\{\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|,\ \ \left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|\right\}}_{\geq 1}-\,k\geq\underbrace{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}1}_{=k}-\,k=0
.)

•

We define the
poise
of
f
to be the nonnegative integer

\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right):=\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|=\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right|\right).

Example 5.16
.

Let
\delta=\left(a,b\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(c,d,e\right)
be
two compositions. Then, the poset
U
in Definition
5.15
is

U=\left\{p_{1}<p_{2}\right\}\sqcup\left\{q_{1}<q_{2}<q_{3}\right\}
.
The following maps (written in two-line notation) are stufufufflers for

\delta
and
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&3&4&5\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
2&5&1&3&4\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&2&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&2&2&3\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
2&2&1&2&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&1&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&1&2\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&1&1&2\end{array}\right).

(The list is not exhaustive – there are many more stufufufflers for
\delta

and
\varepsilon
.)

On the other hand, here are some maps (in two-line notation) that are not
stufufufflers for
\delta
and
\varepsilon
:

•

The map
\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&1&1&1\end{array}\right)
is not a stufufuffler, since it violates
(
99
) for
s=1
.

•

The map
\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&2&1&2\end{array}\right)
is not a stufufuffler, since it is not weakly increasing (
f\left(q_{1}\right)>f\left(q_{2}\right)
).

•

The map
\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
2&2&2&2&2\end{array}\right)
is not a stufufuffler, since it fails to be surjective onto

\left\{1<2<\cdots<k\right\}
whatever
k
is.

Here are the weights of the eight stufufufflers listed above:

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&3&4&5\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a,b,c,d,e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
2&5&1&3&4\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(c,a,d,e,b\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&2&3\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a+b+c,\ d,\ e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&2&2&3\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a,\ b+c+d,\ e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
2&2&1&2&3\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(c,\ a+b+d,\ e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&1&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a+b+c+d+e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&1&1&1&2\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a+b+c+d,\ e\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname*{wt}\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}&q_{3}\\
1&2&1&1&2\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(a+c+d,\ b+e\right).

The losses of these stufufufflers are
0
,
0
,
1
,
1
,
1
,
2
,
1
and

1
, respectively. Their poises are
0
,
0
,
0
,
0
,
0
,
0
,
1
and
1
, respectively.

Intuitively, the composition
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
in
Definition
5.15
can be thought of as a variant of a
stuffle
18
18

18

“Stuffles” are also known
as “overlapping shuffles”; see

[
GriRei20
, Proposition 5.1.3 and Example 5.1.4]
for the meaning of this
concept (and
[
DEMT17
]
for more).
of
\delta
with
\varepsilon
, but
instead of adding an entry of
\delta
with an entry of
\varepsilon
, it
allows adding
i
consecutive entries of
\delta
and
j
consecutive entries
of
\varepsilon
whenever
i
and
j
are integers satisfying
i\approx j
.
(Such a sum can be obtained by starting with
0
and taking turns at adding
the next available entry from
\delta
or from
\varepsilon
; thus the name
“stufufuffle”.) The poise statistic

\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)
tells us how often this
i\approx j

relation becomes an equality. The loss statistic
\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)
tells how much is being added, i.e., how far this
“stufufuffle” deviates from a stuffle.

Now we can restate the multiplication rule for
\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}
in terms of stufufufflers:

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\delta
and
\varepsilon
be two compositions. Then,

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\eta_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

Example 5.18
.

Let
\delta=\left(a,b\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(c,d\right)
be
two compositions of length
2
. Let us compute
\eta_{\left(a,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\left(c,d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}
using
Theorem
5.17
. The stufufufflers for
\delta
and
\varepsilon

are the maps (written here in two-line notation)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&3&4\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&3&2&4\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&4&2&3\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
2&3&1&4\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
2&4&1&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
3&4&1&2\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&2&2\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
2&2&1&2\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&1&1&2\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&1&1\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&1&2\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&1&1&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&1&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&3&1&2\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&3&2&3\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
2&3&1&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
1&2&2&3\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{cccc}p_{1}&p_{2}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
2&3&1&2\end{array}\right).

Their respective weights are

\displaystyle\left(a,b,c,d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a,c,b,d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a,c,d,b\right),

\displaystyle\left(c,a,b,d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(c,a,d,b\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(c,d,a,b\right),

\displaystyle\left(a,\ b+c+d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(c,\ a+b+d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a+b+c,\ d\right),

\displaystyle\left(a+c+d,\ b\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a+c,\ b+d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a+b+c+d\right),

\displaystyle\left(a+c,\ b,\ d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a+c,\ d,\ b\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a,\ c,\ b+d\right),

\displaystyle\left(c,\ a,\ b+d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(a,\ b+c,\ d\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(c,\ a+d,\ b\right);

their respective losses are

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 1,1,1,

\displaystyle 1,0,1,

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

whereas their respective poises are

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 0,0,0,

\displaystyle 0,2,1,

\displaystyle 1,1,1,

\displaystyle 1,1,1.

Thus, Theorem
5.17
yields

\displaystyle\eta_{\left(a,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\left(c,d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\left(a,b,c,d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(a,c,b,d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(a,c,d,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\eta_{\left(c,a,b,d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(c,a,d,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(c,d,a,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -q\eta_{\left(a,\ b+c+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}-q\eta_{\left(c,\ a+b+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}-q\eta_{\left(a+b+c,\ d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -q\eta_{\left(a+c+d,\ b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)^{2}\eta_{\left(a+c,\ b+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}-q\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a+b+c+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a+c,\ b,\ d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a+c,\ d,\ b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a,\ c,\ b+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(c,\ a,\ b+d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a,\ b+c,\ d\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(c,\ a+d,\ b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

Let us now outline how Theorem
5.17
can be derived from Theorem

5.1
.

Proof 5.19

(Proof of Theorem
5.17
(sketched).)
.

Let us define the polynomials

d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(X\right)\in\mathbb{Z}\left[X\right]
as in the proof of Theorem
5.1
. Then, Claim 4 in
said proof shows that

\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}.

(100)

Now, fix a composition
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
. Let
\mathbf{P}
be the set of all pairs

\left(\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\ \left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\right)

satisfying the six conditions

\displaystyle\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname*{Comp};\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp};

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\delta;\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon;

\displaystyle\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 1\text{ for each }s;

\displaystyle\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|

\displaystyle=\alpha_{s}\text{ for each }s.

Then, the equality (
84
) rewrites as

\displaystyle d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\ \left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\right)\in\mathbf{P}}\left(-q\right)^{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(q-1\right)^{\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)}.

(101)

On the other hand, let
\mathbf{S}
be the set of all stufufufflers
f
for

\delta
and
\varepsilon
satisfying
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha
.

We shall construct a bijection
\Phi
from
\mathbf{S}
to
\mathbf{P}
.
Namely,
\Phi
shall send any stufufuffler
f\in\mathbf{S}
to the pair

\left(\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\ \left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\right),

where

\displaystyle\beta_{s}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the composition consisting of the }\delta_{u}\text{ for all }u\in f_{P}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{(in the order of increasing }u\text{)}\vphantom{f_{P}^{-1}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\gamma_{s}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the composition consisting of the }\varepsilon_{v}\text{ for all }v\in f_{Q}^{-1}\left(s\right)\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{(in the order of increasing }v\text{)}\vphantom{f_{P}^{-1}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }s\in\left[k\right].

(We are here using the fact that our stufufuffler
f
must necessarily be a
map from
U
to
\left\{1<2<\cdots<k\right\}
, because its weight

\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha
is a composition of length
k
.)
It is easy to see that this pair really belongs to
\mathbf{P}
, and that

\Phi
is indeed a bijection
19
19

19

Its inverse map
\Phi^{-1}
can easily
be constructed: It sends each pair
\left(\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\ \left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\right)\in\mathbf{P}
to the map
f:U\rightarrow\left[k\right]
that is given by

f\left(p_{u}\right)=\min\left\{s\in\left[k\right]\ \mid\ \ell\left(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{s}\right)\geq u\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }u\in\left[\ell\right]

and

f\left(q_{v}\right)=\min\left\{s\in\left[k\right]\ \mid\ \ell\left(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{s}\right)\geq v\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }v\in\left[m\right].

The idea behind this is that
f\left(p_{u}\right)
is the number
s
such
that the
u
-th entry of the concatenated composition
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}
is taken from its
s
-th factor
\beta_{s}
(and
similarly
f\left(q_{v}\right)
).
.

This bijection
\Phi
has a further useful property: If
\Phi
sends a
stufufuffler
f
to a pair
\left(\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\right),\ \left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)\right)
, then

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{s=1}^{k}\max\left\{\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right),\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right\}-k

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\left(\text{\# of all }s\in\left[k\right]\text{ such that }\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right).

(This is easily seen from the definitions of
\Phi
, of the loss and of the poise.)

Thus, we can use the bijection
\Phi
to rewrite (
101
)
as

\displaystyle d_{\delta,\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{f\in\mathbf{S}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon;\\
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}

(102)

(by the definition of
\mathbf{S}
).

Forget that we fixed
\alpha
. We thus have proved
(
102
) for each composition
\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}
. Hence, we can rewrite (
100
) as

\displaystyle\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a
stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and }\varepsilon;\\
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\right)\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a
stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and }\varepsilon;\\
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\underbrace{\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\eta_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\\
\text{(since
}\alpha=\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon;\\
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)=\alpha\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\eta_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\eta_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}^{\left(q\right)}.

This proves Theorem
5.17
.

5.3
The product rule in terms of subsets

Finally, let us state the product rule for the
\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}
(Theorem
5.1
) in yet another form, using
classical shuffles (
[
GriVas22
, Corollary 1]
):

Definition 5.20
.

If
T
is any set of integers, then
T-1
shall denote the set
\left\{t-1\ \mid\ t\in T\right\}
.

Definition 5.21
.

Let
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\right)
be a
composition with
n
entries. For any
i\in\left[n-1\right]
, we let

\alpha^{\downarrow i}
denote the following composition with
n-1
entries:

\alpha^{\downarrow i}:=\left(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i-1},{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}},\alpha_{i+2},\dots,\alpha_{n}\right).

Furthermore, for any subset
I\subseteq[n-1]
, we set

\alpha^{\downarrow I}:=\left(\left(\cdots\left(\alpha^{\downarrow i_{k}}\right)\cdots\right)^{\downarrow i_{2}}\right)^{\downarrow i_{1}},

where
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}
are the elements of
I
in increasing order.

Finally, if
I
and
J
are two subsets of
[n-1]
, then we set

\alpha^{\downarrow I\downarrow\downarrow J}:=\alpha^{\downarrow K},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{where }K=I\cup J\cup(J-1).

Example 5.22
.

Let
\alpha=\left(a,b,c,d,e,f,g\right)
be a composition with
7
entries.
Then,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\downarrow 2}

\displaystyle=\left(a,\ b+c,\ d,\ e,\ f,\ g\right);

\displaystyle\alpha^{\downarrow\left\{2,4,5\right\}}

\displaystyle=\left(a,\ b+c,\ d+e+f,\ g\right);

\displaystyle\alpha^{\downarrow\left\{2\right\}\downarrow\downarrow\left\{6\right\}}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\downarrow\left\{2,5,6\right\}}=\left(a,\ b+c,\ d,\ e+f+g\right).

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
\delta=\left(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{n}\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m}\right)
be two compositions.

If
T
is any
m
-element subset of
\left[n+m\right]
, then we define the

T
-shuffle
of
\delta
with
\varepsilon
to be the composition

\delta\left\lfloor T\right\rfloor\varepsilon:=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{n+m}\right),

where

\gamma_{k}:=\begin{cases}\delta_{i},&\text{if }k\text{ is the }i\text{-th smallest element of
}\left[n+m\right]\setminus T;\\
\varepsilon_{j},&\text{if }k\text{ is the }j\text{-th smallest element of
}T.\end{cases}

Furthermore, if
T
is any subset of
\left[n+m\right]
, then we define a
further subset

T^{\prime}:=\left(T\setminus\left(T-1\right)\right)\setminus\left\{n+m\right\}.

Then,

\eta_{\delta}^{(q)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{(q)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{triples
}\left(T,I,J\right);\\
T\subseteq\left[n+m\right];\\
\left|T\right|=m;\\
I\subseteq T^{\prime};\\
J\subseteq T^{\prime}\setminus\left\{1\right\};\\
I\cap J=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\left|J\right|}\left(q-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}\eta_{\left(\delta\left\lfloor T\right\rfloor\varepsilon\right)^{\downarrow I\downarrow\downarrow J}}^{\left(q\right)}.

Example 5.24
.

Let
\delta=\left(a\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(b,c\right)
be two
compositions. Then, applying Theorem
5.23
(with
n=1
and

m=2
), we see that
\eta_{\delta}^{(q)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{(q)}=\eta_{\left(a\right)}^{(q)}\eta_{\left(b,c\right)}^{(q)}
is a sum over all triples

\left(T,I,J\right)
satisfying

T\subseteq\left[3\right],\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|T\right|=2,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ I\subseteq T^{\prime},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ J\subseteq T^{\prime}\setminus\left\{1\right\},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ I\cap J=\varnothing.

There are exactly six such triples
\left(T,I,J\right)
, namely

\displaystyle\left(\left\{1,2\right\},\ \varnothing,\ \varnothing\right),\ \left(\left\{1,2\right\},\ \varnothing,\ \left\{2\right\}\right),\ \left(\left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{2\right\},\ \varnothing\right),

\displaystyle\left(\left\{1,3\right\},\ \varnothing,\ \varnothing\right),\ \left(\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{1\right\},\ \varnothing\right),\ \left(\left\{2,3\right\},\ \varnothing,\ \varnothing\right).

Thus, the claim of Theorem
5.23
becomes

\eta_{\left(a\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\left(b,c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}=\eta_{\left(b,c,a\right)}^{\left(q\right)}-q\eta_{\left(a+b+c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(b,\ a+c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(b,a,c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a+b,\ c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(a,b,c\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

(here, we have listed the addends in the same order in which the corresponding
triples were listed above).

Theorem
5.23
can be derived from Theorem
5.17
by
constructing a bijection between the stufufufflers of
\delta
and

\varepsilon
and the triples
\left(T,I,J\right)
from Theorem

5.23
. The details of this bijection are somewhat bothersome,
so we shall omit them, not least because Theorem
5.23
can also
be proved in a different way (using enriched
P
-partitions). The latter proof
has been outlined in
[
GriVas22
, Corollary 1]
and will be elaborated upon
in forthcoming work.

6
Applications

We shall now discuss some applications of the basis
\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{Comp}}
and its features.

6.1
Hopf subalgebras of
\operatorname*{QSym}

The
q=1
case in particular is useful for constructing Hopf subalgebras of

\operatorname*{QSym}
, such as the peak subalgebra
\Pi
introduced by
Stembridge
[
Stembr97
, §3]
and later studied by various authors
(
[
AgBeSo14
, §6, particularly Proposition 6.5]
,
[
BMSW99
]
,

[
BMSW00
, §5]
,
[
Hsiao07
]
etc.). We shall now briefly survey some
Hopf subalgebras that can be obtained in this way.

Convention 6.1
.

For the rest of Subsection
6.1
, we fix a set
T
of
compositions (i.e., a subset
T
of
\operatorname*{Comp}
).

We let
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
be the

\mathbf{k}
-submodule of
\operatorname*{QSym}
spanned by the family

\left(\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\right)_{\alpha\in T}
.

When is this
\mathbf{k}
-submodule
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
a subcoalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
? The answer is
simple:
20
20

20

We are being sloppy: For us here, a “subcoalgebra” of a coalgebra
C
means a
\mathbf{k}
-submodule
D
of
C
that satisfies

\Delta\left(D\right)\subseteq\left(\text{image of the canonical map
}D\otimes D\rightarrow C\otimes C\right).

This is
not
the algebraically literate definition of a
“subcoalgebra”, as it does not imply that

D
itself becomes a
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra (after all, the canonical map

D\otimes D\rightarrow C\otimes C
might fail to be injective, and then it is
not clear how to “restrict”
\Delta
to a
map
D\rightarrow D\otimes D
). Fortunately, the two definitions are
equivalent when
\mathbf{k}
is a field (or when
D
is a direct addend of
C

as a
\mathbf{k}
-module).

Proposition 6.1
.

For any subset
Y
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
, we let

\displaystyle Y^{\ast}:=\

\displaystyle\left\{\text{all compositions whose entries all belong to
}Y\right\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)\in\operatorname*{Comp}\ \mid\ \alpha_{i}\in Y\text{ for each }i\right\}.

(a)

If
T=Y^{\ast}
for some subset
Y
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
, then
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a subcoalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

(b)

If
\mathbf{k}
is a field and
r\neq 0
, then the converse
holds as well: If
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is
a subcoalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
, then
T=Y^{\ast}
for some subset

Y
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
.

Proof 6.2

(Proof sketch.)
.

(a)
This follows from Theorem
3.28
.

(b)
Use the graded dual
\operatorname*{NSym}
of

\operatorname*{QSym}
and Proposition
4.15
. (The
orthogonal complement of a subcoalgebra is an ideal.)

Proposition
6.1
allows us to restrict ourselves to
sets
T
of the form
Y^{\ast}
for
Y\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
if we want
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
to be a
Hopf subalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. However, not every set
T
of this
form
Y^{\ast}
results in a Hopf subalgebra. For generic
q
, this happens
fairly rarely:

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
Y
be a subset of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
that is closed under addition (i.e., satisfies
y+z\in Y
for every
y,z\in Y
). Let
T:=Y^{\ast}
. Then,
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
.

Proof 6.4

(Proof sketch.)
.

Clearly,
1=\eta_{\varnothing}^{\left(q\right)}\in\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
, and Proposition

6.1

(a)
shows that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a subcoalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. Next, we will show that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is closed under multiplication. In view of Theorem

5.1
, this will follow once we can show the following claim:

Claim 1:
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\delta\in Y^{\ast}
and

\varepsilon\in Y^{\ast}
be two compositions all of whose entries are
\in Y
. Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp}
and

\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp}
be
2k

compositions satisfying

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\delta\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon

\displaystyle\text{and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)

\displaystyle>0\text{ for all }s.

Then,

\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\in Y^{\ast}.

Proof 6.5

(Proof of Claim 1.)
.

We need to show that
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|\in Y
for each
s\in\left[k\right]
. To
do so, we fix
s\in\left[k\right]
. Then,
\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)>0
(by assumption). In other words, at least
one of the compositions
\beta_{s}
and
\gamma_{s}
is nonempty.

However, all entries of the composition
\beta_{s}
are entries of the
composition
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}=\delta
, and thus belong to
Y

(since
\delta\in Y^{\ast}
). Thus, the sum of all entries of
\beta_{s}

either equals
0
or belongs to
Y
(since
Y
is closed under addition). In
other words, the size
\left|\beta_{s}\right|
either equals
0
or
belongs to
Y
. Similarly,
\left|\gamma_{s}\right|
either equals

0
or belongs to
Y
. Hence, the sum
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|
either equals
0
or belongs to
Y
as
well (since
Y
is closed under addition). Since
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|
cannot equal
0
(because
at least one of the compositions
\beta_{s}
and
\gamma_{s}
is nonempty), we
thus conclude that
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|
belongs to
Y
. In other words,
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|\in Y
. As we said, this
completes the proof of Claim 1.

Now, Claim 1 (together with
1\in\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
) shows that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-subalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. As we saw above,

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-subcoalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
as well, and thus is a
\mathbf{k}
-subbialgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. This bialgebra
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is connected graded, and therefore a Hopf
algebra (by Takeuchi’s famous result
[
GriRei20
, Proposition 1.4.16]
). The
inclusion map
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}\rightarrow\operatorname*{QSym}
is a bialgebra morphism between two Hopf
algebras, and thus automatically a Hopf algebra morphism (by another
well-known result:
[
GriRei20
, Corollary 1.4.27]
). Hence,

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. This proves Proposition

6.3
.

Example 6.6
.

The subset
\left\{2,4,6,8,\ldots\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
is closed under addition. Thus, Proposition

6.3
shows that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
for
Y:=\left\{2,4,6,8,\ldots\right\}
and

T:=Y^{\ast}
. This Hopf subalgebra can be viewed as a copy of

\operatorname*{QSym}
in the indeterminates
x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2},\ldots
, and thus is isomorphic to
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

Example 6.7
.

The subset
\left\{2,3,4,5,\ldots\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
is closed under addition. Thus, Proposition

6.3
shows that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
for
Y:=\left\{2,3,4,5,\ldots\right\}
and

T:=Y^{\ast}
.

Proposition
6.3
is not very surprising. In fact,
(
5
) shows that (under the assumptions of Proposition

6.3
) the space
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is just the
\mathbf{k}
-linear span of
the functions
r^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}M_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in Y^{\ast}
; but the latter span is easily seen to be a Hopf subalgebra (using

[
GriRei20
, Proposition 5.1.3]
and (
38
)).

If
q\neq 1
and if
r
is invertible, then Proposition

6.3
has a converse (i.e.,
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(q\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
only when
Y
is closed under addition), since it is
easy to see that

\eta_{\left(a\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\left(b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}=\left(q-1\right)\eta_{\left(a+b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(a,b\right)}^{\left(q\right)}+\eta_{\left(b,a\right)}^{\left(q\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
any }a,b\geq 1.

However, more interesting behavior emerges when
q=1
:

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
Y
be a subset of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
that is closed under ternary addition (i.e., satisfies

y+z+w\in Y
for every
y,z,w\in Y
). Let
T:=Y^{\ast}
. Then,

\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(1\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra
of
\operatorname*{QSym}
.

Proof 6.9

(Proof sketch.)
.

This is similar to Proposition

6.3
, but now we set
q=1
and observe that all
addends on the right hand side of Theorem
5.1
that satisfy

\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)=\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for at least one }s\in\left[k\right]

are
0
(because they include the factor
\left(1-1\right)^{\text{a
positive integer}}
, which vanishes), and all the remaining addends have the
property that
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|=1
for all
s
(since
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|\leq 1
and

\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)\neq\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)
). Hence,
the following claim now replaces Claim 1:

Claim 1’:
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\delta\in Y^{\ast}
and

\varepsilon\in Y^{\ast}
be two compositions all of whose entries are
\in Y
. Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp}
and

\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname*{Comp}
be
2k

compositions satisfying

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\delta\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{k}=\varepsilon

\displaystyle\text{and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=1\text{ for all }s.

Then,

\left(\left|\beta_{1}\right|+\left|\gamma_{1}\right|,\ \left|\beta_{2}\right|+\left|\gamma_{2}\right|,\ \ldots,\ \left|\beta_{k}\right|+\left|\gamma_{k}\right|\right)\in Y^{\ast}.

Proof 6.10

(Proof of Claim 1’.)
.

We need to show that
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|\in Y
for each
s\in\left[k\right]
. To
do so, we fix
s\in\left[k\right]
. Then,
\left|\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)-\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)\right|=1
(by
assumption), and thus
\ell\left(\beta_{s}\right)+\ell\left(\gamma_{s}\right)
is odd. Hence,
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|
is a sum of an odd number of entries of
\delta
and

\varepsilon
, and therefore a sum of an odd number of elements of
Y
(since

\delta
and
\varepsilon
belong to
Y^{\ast}
). But
Y
is closed under
ternary addition, and therefore any sum of an odd number of elements of
Y

must belong to
Y
(easy induction exercise). Hence,
\left|\beta_{s}\right|+\left|\gamma_{s}\right|\in Y
, and thus Claim 1’ is proved.

The rest of the proof proceeds as for Proposition

6.3
.

Example 6.11
.

The subset
\left\{1,3,5,7,\ldots\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
is closed under ternary addition. Thus, Proposition

6.8
shows that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(1\right)}
is a Hopf subalgebra of

\operatorname*{QSym}
for
Y:=\left\{1,3,5,7,\ldots\right\}
and

T:=Y^{\ast}
. This Hopf subalgebra is precisely the peak algebra
\Pi
of

[
Stembr97
, §3]
,
[
AgBeSo14
, §6, particularly Proposition 6.5]
,

[
BMSW99
]
,
[
BMSW00
, §5]
and
[
Hsiao07
]
(since
[
Hsiao07
, (2.1) and
(2.2)]
shows that the
\theta_{\alpha}
for
\alpha
odd have the
same span as the
\eta_{\alpha}
for
\alpha
odd, but
[
Hsiao07
, Proposition
2.1]
shows that the latter
\eta_{\alpha}
are precisely our

\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(1\right)}
up to sign).

Example 6.12
.

The subset
\left\{\text{positive integers }\neq 2\right\}=\left\{1,3,4,5,\ldots\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
is closed under
ternary addition. Thus, Proposition
6.8
shows
that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(1\right)}
is a Hopf
subalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
for
Y:=\left\{\text{positive integers
}\neq 2\right\}
and
T:=Y^{\ast}
. This Hopf subalgebra is the Hopf
subalgebra
\Xi
constructed in
[
BMSW00
, Theorem 5.7]
. (Indeed, both Hopf
subalgebras have the same orthogonal complement: the principal ideal of

\operatorname*{NSym}
generated by
\eta_{2}^{\ast}=\dfrac{1}{4}X_{2}=\dfrac{1}{4}\left(2H_{2}-H_{1}H_{1}\right)
.)

Example 6.13
.

More generally, if we pick a positive integer
k
and set

Y:=\left\{\text{odd positive integers}\right\}\cup\left\{k,k+1,k+2,\ldots\right\}

and
T:=Y^{\ast}
, then Proposition
6.8
shows
that
\operatorname*{QSym}\nolimits_{T}^{\left(1\right)}
is a Hopf
subalgebra of
\operatorname*{QSym}
(since
Y
is closed under ternary addition).

The reader can find more examples without trouble. When
\mathbf{k}
is
nontrivial and
2
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
, Proposition

6.8
is easily seen to have a converse (using
Example
5.3
).

6.2
A new shuffle algebra

Next, we shall use the enriched
q
-monomial quasisymmetric functions to
realize a certain deformed version of the shuffle product, which has appeared
in recent work of
[
BoNoTh22
]
by Bouillot, Novelli and Thibon
(generalizing the “block shuffle product” of Hirose and Sato
[
HirSat22
,
\Diamond
]
).

Shuffle products are a broad and deep subject with a long history and many
applications (e.g., to multiple zeta values, algebraic topology and stochastic
differential equations). An overview of known variants (such as the stuffles,
the “muffles”, the infiltrations and many
more) can be found in
[
DEMT17
, Table 1]
. In the following, we shall
discuss a variant that does not directly fit into the framework of

[
DEMT17
]
, but is sufficiently similar to enjoy some of the same behavior.
To our knowledge, this variant has first appeared in
[
BoNoTh22
]
. We will
use the letters
a
and
b
for what was called
\alpha
and
\beta
in

[
BoNoTh22
]
, as we prefer to use Greek letters for compositions.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the free
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with generators

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
. It has a basis consisting of all words over the
alphabet
\left\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right\}
; these words are in
bijection with the compositions. In fact, let us set

x_{\gamma}:=x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots x_{\gamma_{k}}

(103)

for every composition
\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\right)
. Then, the bijection sends each composition
\gamma
to the
word
x_{\gamma}
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\zeta_{k}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

be the
\mathbf{k}
-linear operator defined by

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}\left(1\right)

\displaystyle=0;

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}\left(x_{i}w\right)

\displaystyle=x_{i+k}w\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
each }i\geq 1\text{ and any word }w.

(Thus, explicitly, the map
\zeta_{k}
sends
1
to
0
, and transforms any
nonempty word by adding
k
to the subscript of its first letter. For example,

\zeta_{k}\left(x_{u}x_{v}x_{w}\right)=x_{u+k}x_{v}x_{w}
for any

u,v,w\geq 1
.)

Fix two elements
a
and
b
of the base ring
\mathbf{k}
.

Let
\#:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
be the

\mathbf{k}
-bilinear map on
\mathcal{F}
defined recursively by the
requirements

\displaystyle 1\#w

\displaystyle=w\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any word }w;

\displaystyle w\#1

\displaystyle=w\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any word }w;

\displaystyle\left(x_{i}u\right)\#\left(x_{j}v\right)

\displaystyle=x_{i}\left(u\#\left(x_{j}v\right)\right)+x_{j}\left(\left(x_{i}u\right)\#v\right)+ax_{i+j}\left(u\#v\right)+b\zeta_{i+j}\left(u\#v\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }i,j\geq 1\text{ and
any words }u\text{ and }v\text{.}

We call this bilinear map
\#
the
stufufuffle
21
21

21

This is a riff
on the notion of “stuffle” (which is
recovered when
a=1
and
b=0
) and the fact that multiple letters of both
words
u
and
v
can get combined into one in
u\#v
.
. Explicitly, we can
compute this operation as follows:

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
\delta=\left(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}\right)
and
\varepsilon=\left(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m}\right)
be two compositions.
Then, using the notation of (
103
), we have

x_{\delta}\#x_{\varepsilon}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and }\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}.

Proof 6.15

(Proof sketch.)
.

Use strong induction on
\ell+m
.

Induction step:
If
\delta=\varnothing
or
\varepsilon=\varnothing
,
then the claim is easy to check. Thus, assume WLOG that neither
\delta
nor

\varepsilon
is
\varnothing
. Let
i=\delta_{1}
and
j=\varepsilon_{1}
and

\overline{\delta}=\left(\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}\right)
and
\overline{\varepsilon}=\left(\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3},\ldots,\varepsilon_{m}\right)
. Hence,
x_{\delta}=x_{i}x_{\overline{\delta}}
and
x_{\varepsilon}=x_{j}x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}
, so that

\displaystyle x_{\delta}\#x_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left(x_{i}x_{\overline{\delta}}\right)\#\left(x_{j}x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle=x_{i}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#\underbrace{\left(x_{j}x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)}_{=x_{\varepsilon}}\right)+x_{j}\left(\underbrace{\left(x_{i}x_{\overline{\delta}}\right)}_{=x_{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+ax_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+b\zeta_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the recursive
definition of }\#\right)

\displaystyle=x_{i}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\varepsilon}\right)+x_{j}\left(x_{\delta}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+ax_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+b\zeta_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right).

(104)

On the other hand, the stufufufflers
f
for
\delta
and
\varepsilon
can be
classified into four types:

1.

Type 1
consists of those stufufufflers
f
that satisfy

\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=1
and
\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=0
(so that the composition

\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
begins with the entry
\delta_{1}=i
).

2.

Type 2
consists of those stufufufflers
f
that satisfy

\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=0
and
\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=1
(so that the composition

\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
begins with the entry
\varepsilon_{1}=j
).

3.

Type 3
consists of those stufufufflers
f
that satisfy

\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=1
and
\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|=1
(so that the composition

\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
begins with the entry
\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}=i+j
).

4.

Type 4
consists of those stufufufflers
f
that satisfy

\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|+\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|>2
(so that both numbers
\left|f_{P}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|
and
\left|f_{Q}^{-1}\left(1\right)\right|
are positive
22
22

22

by
(
99
), applied to
s=1
, and one of them is
at least
2
, and therefore the composition
\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)
begins with the entry
\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}+\left(\text{some further numbers}\right)
).

A type-1 stufufuffler
f
for
\delta
and
\varepsilon
becomes a
stufufuffler for
\overline{\delta}
and
\varepsilon
if we decrease all its
values by
1
and remove
p_{1}
from
P
. This is furthermore a bijection
from
\left\{\text{type-1 stufufufflers for }\delta\text{ and }\varepsilon\right\}
to
\left\{\text{stufufufflers for }\overline{\delta}\text{ and }\varepsilon\right\}
, and this bijection preserves both loss
and poise while removing the first entry from the weight. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a type-1 stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{
and }\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\overline{\delta}\text{ and }\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{i}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\cdot\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\overline{\delta}\text{ and }\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\varepsilon}\\
\text{(by the induction hypothesis,}\\
\text{since
}\overline{\delta}\text{ has length }\ell-1<\ell\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\varepsilon}\right).

Similar reasoning leads to

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a type-2 stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=x_{j}\left(x_{\delta}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right);

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a type-3 stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=ax_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right);

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a type-4 stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=b\zeta_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right).

Adding these four equalities together (and recalling that each stufufuffler
for
\delta
and
\varepsilon
belongs to exactly one of the four types 1, 2,
3 and 4), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\varepsilon}\right)+x_{j}\left(x_{\delta}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+ax_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right)+b\zeta_{i+j}\left(x_{\overline{\delta}}\#x_{\overline{\varepsilon}}\right).

Comparing this with (
104
), we obtain

x_{\delta}\#x_{\varepsilon}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and }\varepsilon\end{subarray}}b^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}a^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}x_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}.

This completes the induction step, and thus Proposition

6.14
is proved.

Theorem 6.16
.

The bilinear map
\#
is commutative and associative,
and the element
1\in\mathcal{F}
is a neutral element for it. Thus, the

\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{F}
, equipped with the operation
\#
(as
multiplication), becomes a commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with unity
1
.

It appears possible to prove Theorem
6.16
by induction, but
the most convenient method at this point is to deduce this from the properties
of the enriched
q
-monomial basis of
\operatorname*{QSym}
. To wit, the
following proposition connects the map
\#
to the latter basis:

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
q
and
u
be two elements of
\mathbf{k}
such
that
a=\left(q-1\right)u
and
b=-qu^{2}
. (Such
q
and
u
do not
always exist, of course.)

Let
\operatorname*{eta}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\operatorname*{QSym}
be the

\mathbf{k}
-linear map that sends the word
x_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha_{1}}x_{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{\alpha_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}
to
u^{\ell\left(\alpha\right)}\eta_{\alpha}^{\left(q\right)}\in\operatorname*{QSym}
for
each composition
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right)
. Then,
\operatorname*{eta}\left(g\#h\right)=\left(\operatorname*{eta}g\right)\cdot\left(\operatorname*{eta}h\right)
for
any
g,h\in\mathcal{F}
.

Proof 6.18

(Proof sketch.)
.

Let
g,h\in\mathcal{F}
. We WLOG assume that
g=x_{\delta}
and

h=x_{\varepsilon}
for two compositions
\delta
and
\varepsilon
. Consider
these
\delta
and
\varepsilon
. Thus,

\operatorname*{eta}g=\operatorname*{eta}x_{\delta}=u^{\ell\left(\delta\right)}\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }\operatorname*{eta}\right)

and similarly
\operatorname*{eta}h=u^{\ell\left(\varepsilon\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}
. Multiplying these two equalities, we
find

\displaystyle\left(\operatorname*{eta}g\right)\cdot\left(\operatorname*{eta}h\right)

\displaystyle=u^{\ell\left(\delta\right)}\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\cdot u^{\ell\left(\varepsilon\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}=u^{\ell\left(\delta\right)+\ell\left(\varepsilon\right)}\eta_{\delta}^{\left(q\right)}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\left(q\right)}

\displaystyle=u^{\ell\left(\delta\right)+\ell\left(\varepsilon\right)}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
\text{for }\delta\text{ and
}\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\left(-q\right)^{\operatorname*{loss}\left(f\right)}\left(q-1\right)^{\operatorname*{poise}\left(f\right)}\eta_{\operatorname*{wt}\left(f\right)}^{\left(q\right)}

(105)

(by Theorem
5.17
).

On the other hand, from
g=x_{\delta}
and
h=x_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

g\#h=x_{\delta}\#x_{\varepsilon}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\text{ is a stufufuffler}\\
